> DEVELOPER

Why not just port all this to the edge functions?

> AGENT

Honest answer: **you can port most of it, but not all — and the stuff that can't port is exactly the stuff that causes the edge-function timeout problem anyway.**

## What can and can't move into the edge function

| Script | Real dependency | Port viable? |
|---|---|---|
| `gemini_picks.py` / `gemini_refine.py` / `gemini_review.py` | Gemini REST API | ✅ trivial — just `fetch()` with an API key |
| `remix.py` | likely another LLM wrapper | ✅ trivial |
| `transcribe.py` | Whisper | ✅ if you swap local whisper for the OpenAI Whisper API — a REST call, works in an edge function |
| `cut.py` (EDL assembly only) | pure data transform | ✅ porting the logic is easy |
| `scenes.py` | **PySceneDetect + ffmpeg** | ❌ no ffmpeg in Deno/edge |
| `shots.py` | **ffmpeg frame extraction** | ❌ same |
| `cut.py` (render step) | **ffmpeg concat/encode** | ❌ same |

Deno edge functions have no ffmpeg, no native binaries, no C-library bindings, and ~50s soft / 90s hard timeouts plus ~1GB memory. Rendering a 30-second hype cut from a 1-hour source would blow all three limits even if ffmpeg were available. This isn't a Supabase quirk — it's true of Cloudflare Workers, Vercel Edge, all edge runtimes.

## The real choice: hybrid shape

Given the above, the architecture is actually:

**Port to edge function (no sidecar needed):**
- All Gemini/LLM scripts → TS tool handlers
- Whisper → call OpenAI Whisper API directly
- EDL assembly → pure function
- Any metadata/orchestration logic

**Outside the edge function (the ffmpeg-dependent ones):**
Three realistic options, pick one —
1. **Sidecar service** (Railway/Fly container running Python + ffmpeg). Matches today's scripts. Lowest porting cost.
2. **A Node service with ffmpeg binary** (Railway, or Supabase's long-running background functions if/when those stabilize). Unifies the runtime but still a separate service.
3. **Commercial video APIs** (Mux, Twelve Labs, Cloudinary). No self-hosted processing. Costs more per-call but zero infra.

Any of these is called the same way from the edge-function tool: `fire async job → store id → poll → return result`. Same pattern as `create_task` today.

## My revised recommendation

**Phase 1, all-in edge function:**
- Port the 4 LLM-based tools today. That's most of the value (`gemini_picks`, `gemini_refine`, `gemini_review`, `remix`).
- Add Whisper API transcription as a synchronous tool (short clips) or async job (long clips).
- Ship and use it. See how often you actually need ffmpeg-dependent tools.

**Phase 2, only if Phase 1 shows you need it:**
- Add ffmpeg-dependent tools via whichever of the three options fits your cost/ops tolerance.

This defers the sidecar decision, keeps the scope tight, and gets you 60-70% of the tool value with zero new infrastructure. The "port everything" instinct is right for the majority — it's just physically blocked for the video-encoding path.

but should this just be a service that we use?

> AGENT

Yes — and I think you've landed on the right shape. Let me be clear about what that actually looks like.

## One service, clean split of concerns

**The tools service** (Python, Railway/Fly/similar, ~1 small container):
- All 10 banodoco scripts, wrapped as HTTP endpoints
- POST `/transcribe`, `/detect-scenes`, `/sample-shots`, `/pick-highlights`, `/assemble-cut`, etc.
- Short jobs respond synchronously; long ones return a `job_id`, write progress + results to Supabase storage/tables, caller polls
- Keeps Python because the video/audio libraries (whisper, PySceneDetect, ffmpeg via moviepy) are Python-native and rewriting them in TS is a waste

**The edge function** (unchanged, minus the hybrid noise):
- Still runs the agent loop: Groq triage → Kimi/Claude
- Still owns timeline mutations (`insert_clips_from_edl`, `run`, etc.) because those need the Supabase context
- Each "external" tool handler in `tools/` becomes a thin `fetch(TOOLS_SERVICE_URL, ...)` wrapper
- No ffmpeg, no Python, no runtime hybrid

## What you gain by making it a proper service (not a sidecar)

1. **It's reusable beyond the agent.** The same service can power: a CLI you run locally, a scheduled job that processes uploaded videos overnight, a dashboard button that says "auto-cut highlights," a future mobile flow. If it lived inside the edge function or a half-embedded sidecar, none of that works.
2. **Clear ownership boundary.** "Video work" lives in one repo/service, easy to reason about. Upgrades to whisper or ffmpeg don't ripple into the agent code.
3. **Independent scaling.** Transcription workloads can run on a container with more CPU/memory than an edge function would ever get. Gemini-heavy workloads can have their own rate-limit budget.
4. **Testable in isolation.** Unit-test transcribe + picks + cut as a pipeline without mocking the edge function or Supabase.

## Honest tradeoffs

- **One more service to deploy/monitor.** But you're already running `reigh-megaplan-dev` on Railway; adding a second container is cheap operationally.
- **Network hop per tool call.** Fine for jobs measured in seconds-to-minutes (most of these). Not fine for millisecond-hot paths (none of these are).
- **Auth**: service gates on a shared secret the edge function holds. Simple. Rotate via env var.

## Concrete recommendation

Build it as `reigh-video-tools` on Railway (or wherever):
- FastAPI (~150 lines) that imports the existing scripts and exposes them as endpoints
- Results written to Supabase storage + a `video_tool_jobs` table for poll status
- Internal `.mp4`/`.json` working directory scoped per-timeline or per-job
- Same container hosts a `/render` endpoint if/when you add Remotion cloud-style rendering

The edge function changes are minimal: add 10 tool schemas + 10 tiny handlers that each forward to the service. Agent prompting is unchanged conceptually — it just has more tools to call.

This is genuinely a cleaner design than what I proposed before. Good push.

On your new direction — **making the banodoco tools into a service the agent calls**: that matches what I landed on as the right shape in the last message.
